Deep Bootstrap
Jinyuan Chang, Yuling Jiao, Lican Kang, Junjie Shi

Abstract
In this work, we propose a novel deep bootstrap framework for nonparametric regression based on conditional diffusion models. Specifically, we construct a conditional diffusion model to learn the distribution of the response variable given the covariates. This model is then used to generate bootstrap samples by pairing the original covariates with newly synthesized responses. We reformulate nonparametric regression as conditional sample mean estimation, which is implemented directly via the learned conditional diffusion model. Unlike traditional bootstrap methods that decouple the estimation of the conditional distribution, sampling, and nonparametric regression, our approach integrates these components into a unified generative framework. With the expressive capacity of diffusion models, our method facilitates both efficient sampling from high-dimensional or multimodal distributions and accurate nonparametric estimation. We establish rigorous theoretical guarantees for the proposed method. In particular, we derive optimal end-to-end convergence rates in the Wasserstein distance between the learned and target conditional distributions. Building on this foundation, we further establish the convergence guarantees of the resulting bootstrap procedure. Numerical studies demonstrate the effectiveness and scalability of our approach for complex regression tasks. KEY WORDS : Bootstrap, Statistical inference, Conditional diffusion model, End-to-end convergence rate.



1
Introduction

Across many modern scientific domains, inferential goals have extended far beyond point estimation to the construction of confidence intervals and other measures of uncertainty, which are indispensable for assessing estimator variability and supporting reliable scientific and operational decisions.
Such needs arise in areas ranging from biomedical research
(
Pencina and D’agostino 2004
;
Benjamini and Yekutieli 2005
)
and genomics
(
Efron et al. 2001
;
Storey and Tibshirani 2003
)
, to environmental science
(
Tebaldi and Knutti 2007
;
North et al. 2011
)
, econometrics
(
Stock and Yogo 2002
;
Paparoditis and Politis 2003
)
, and computer-model calibration
(
Kennedy and O’Hagan 2001
)
. By providing a confidence range for parameter estimates, interval estimation mitigates the risks of real-world model deployment, facilitates the practical application of data science to interdisciplinary problems, and fosters robust decision-making
(
Kirch et al. 2025
)
. As a result, the demands for interval estimation, and consequently for its validity and precision, have experienced a sustained increase over time and are reflected in a number of recent studies. For example, in proteomics, confidence intervals are employed to assess the association between post-translational modifications and intrinsically disordered regions of proteins, validating hypotheses derived from predictive models and facilitating large-scale functional analyses
(
Tunyasuvunakool et al. 2021
;
Bludau et al. 2022
)
. In genomic research, confidence intervals are leveraged to characterize the distribution of gene expression levels, enabling robust inferences about promoter sequence effects and genetic variability
(
Vaishnav et al. 2022
)
. In the realm of environmental science, interval estimation can be used to monitor deforestation rates of forests, yielding uncertainty-aware insights critical for climate policy formulation
(
Bullock et al. 2020
)
. As for social sciences, confidence intervals are utilized to evaluate relationships between socioeconomic factors, bolstering the robustness of conclusions drawn from census data
(
Ding et al. 2021
)
.
In these contexts, analytic characterization of sampling distributions is often infeasible, underscoring the enduring importance of bootstrap methodology
(
Efron 1979
)
, whose model-agnostic resampling framework provides a versatile and practically reliable foundation for uncertainty quantification in contemporary statistical applications.

The Bootstrap method, originally proposed by
Efron 1979
, has become a foundational tool in modern statistical inference. It is widely used for estimating standard errors, constructing confidence intervals, correcting bias, and evaluating model performance.
The core idea of the Bootstrap is to approximate the sampling distribution of a statistic by repeatedly resampling, with replacement, from the observed data to generate multiple resampled datasets. This resampling-based approach is entirely data-driven and nonparametric, requiring no strong assumptions about the underlying population distribution. As such, it bypasses the derivation of asymptotic distributions, which are often difficult or infeasible to obtain in complex or nonstandard settings, and thus provides substantial flexibility and broad applicability.
In particular, Bootstrap methods have found extensive applications in nonparametric regression analysis

(
Freedman 1981
;
Silverman and Young 1987
;
Härdle and Bowman 1988
;
Härdle and Marron 1991
;
Rutherford and Yakowitz 1991
;
Hall 1992
;
Härdle and Mammen 1993
)
.
Beyond estimating confidence intervals for regression coefficients in parametric models,
these methods have been successfully extended to nonparametric regression models, enabling rigorous uncertainty quantification for the regression function itself.
For a comprehensive introduction of bootstrap theory and methods, see the related books
(
Efron and Tibshirani 1994
;
Shao and Tu 2012
;
Hall 2013
)
.
We now briefly review the application of bootstrap methods in nonparametric regression. Consider the nonparametric regression model:

\mathbf{Y}=f_{0}(\mathbf{X})+\epsilon,

where
\mathbf{Y}\in\mathbb{R}
is the response variable,
\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathcal{X}}}
is the covariate vector, and
\epsilon
is a random error term satisfying
\mathbb{E}[\epsilon|\mathbf{X}]=0
and
\operatorname{Var}(\epsilon)<\infty
. The regression function is defined as
f_{0}(\mathbf{x}):=\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{x}]
,
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathcal{X}}}
.
We assume access to an independent and
identically distributed (i.i.d) dataset

\mathcal{D}:=\{(\mathbf{X}_{i},\mathbf{Y}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
drawn from the joint distribution
P_{\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}}=P_{\mathbf{X}}\times P_{\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X}}
of the
covariate-response pair
(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})
.
Here,
P_{\mathbf{X}}
denotes
the marginal distribution of the covariate
\mathbf{X}
,
and
P_{\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X}}
denotes the conditional distribution of
\mathbf{Y}
given
\mathbf{X}
.
In bootstrap methods for nonparametric regression, we first obtain an estimator
\widehat{f}
of the underlying regression
f_{0}
using the original dataset
\mathcal{D}
. This estimator can be constructed using a variety of classical nonparametric methods, such as kernel estimation, local polynomial, splines, wavelets, or nearest-neighbor regression
(
Györfi et al. 2002
;
Wasserman 2006
;
Biau and Devroye 2015
)
, as well as modern approaches based on deep neural networks
(DNNs)

(
Bauer and Kohler 2019
;
Schmidt-Hieber 2020
;
Kohler and Langer 2021
;
Farrell et al. 2021
;
Jiao et al. 2023
;
Bhattacharya et al. 2024
)
.
We note that while classical nonparametric methods often face performance limitations in high-dimensional settings, DNN–based methods have demonstrated superior adaptability and accuracy in such complex scenarios.
Next, we construct a bootstrap dataset
\mathcal{D}^{*}:=\{(\mathbf{X}_{i}^{*},\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{*})\}_{i=1}^{n}\sim P^{*}_{\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}}:=P^{*}_{\mathbf{X}}\times P^{*}_{\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X}}
.
Here,
\mathcal{D}^{*}
consists of independent replicates of the bootstrap covariate-response pair

(\mathbf{X}^{*},\mathbf{Y}^{*})
. The distribution

P^{*}_{\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}}
denotes the joint distribution of

(\mathbf{X}^{*},\mathbf{Y}^{*})
and is an approximation to the true
data-generating distribution
P_{\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}}
. Specifically,
P^{*}_{\mathbf{X}}
represents the marginal distribution of the bootstrap covariate
\mathbf{X}^{*}
, while
P^{*}_{\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X}}
corresponds to the conditional distribution of the bootstrap response
\mathbf{Y}^{*}
given
\mathbf{X}^{*}
.
Various techniques have been proposed to construct such bootstrap datasets, including the residual Bootstrap
(
Freedman 1981
)
, the wild Bootstrap
(
Härdle and Marron 1991
;
Härdle and Mammen 1993
)
, the paired (or naive resampling) Bootstrap, the smooth Bootstrap
(
Silverman and Young 1987
)
,
and the conditional Bootstrap
(
Rutherford and Yakowitz 1991
)
.
A common approach, for instance, is to fix the covariates as
\mathbf{X}_{i}^{*}=\mathbf{X}_{i}
, and sample
\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{*}\sim\widehat{P}_{\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{X}_{i}}
, where
\widehat{P}_{\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X}}
is an estimator of the conditional distribution of
\mathbf{Y}
given
\mathbf{X}
, such as one obtained via conditional kernel density estimation.
Applying the same estimation procedure used for
\widehat{f}
to the bootstrap dataset
\mathcal{D}^{*}
, we obtain a bootstrap estimator
\widehat{f}^{*}
.
This conditional bootstrap framework allows for the derivation of the conditional distribution
\widehat{P}_{\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X}}
, and thus facilitates the construction of confidence intervals for
f_{0}(\mathbf{x})
. However, generating samples from
\widehat{P}_{\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X}}
can be computationally challenging, particularly when the conditional distribution is high-dimensional or multi-modal
(
Dunson and Johndrow 2020
)
.

Existing bootstrap methods for nonparametric regression encompass a variety of resampling techniques and nonparametric estimation approaches. However, these methods face significant challenges, particularly in high-dimensional settings where traditional nonparametric estimators often exhibit degraded performance. Specifically, smooth bootstrap and conditional bootstrap methods require the estimation and sampling from (conditional) distributions, processes that can be hindered by the complexities associated with high-dimensionality and multimodality. As a result, current bootstrap methods for nonparametric regression lack a unified framework that can simultaneously address both resampling and nonparametric estimation in high-dimensional regression settings.
To address these challenges, we propose a deep bootstrap method that unifies nonparametric estimation and conditional distribution learning through a conditional diffusion model
(
Song et al. 2020
)
. By integrating this deep generative modeling framework into the bootstrap procedure for nonparametric regression, our method ensures both efficient sampling and accurate nonparametric estimation, even in high-dimensional and complex settings.
Our approach proceeds in two main stages. First, we construct a conditional diffusion model based on the original dataset
\mathcal{D}
to learn the conditional distribution
\widehat{P}_{\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X}}
, which is the estimator of the true conditional distribution
P_{\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X}}
.
Under certain conditions, we derive a sharp end-to-end convergence rate in Wasserstein distance between

\widehat{P}_{\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X}}
and
P_{\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X}}
.
Using this estimated distribution, we can obtain the nonparametric regression estimator as

\widehat{f}(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\widehat{\mathbf{Y}}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(j)},

where

\{\widehat{\mathbf{Y}}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(j)}\}_{j=1}^{n}
are i.i.d. samples drawn from
\widehat{P}_{\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{x}}
by running the sampling dynamics of the constructed conditional diffusion model.
Here, we use the law of large numbers and the fact that the underlying regression function can be expressed as
f_{0}(\mathbf{x}):=\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{Y}_{\mathbf{x}})
, where
\mathbf{Y}_{\mathbf{x}}\sim P_{\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{x}}
.
Next, we generate a bootstrap dataset
\mathcal{D}^{*}=\{(\mathbf{X}_{i}^{*},\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{*})\}_{i=1}^{n}
, where each response
\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{*}
is sampled from the learned conditional distribution
\widehat{P}_{\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{X}_{i}}
via the conditional diffusion model, while covariates
\mathbf{X}_{i}^{*}
are taken from the original dataset. Based on
\mathcal{D}^{*}
, we compute the bootstrap estimator
\widehat{f}^{*}(\mathbf{x})
using the same nonparametric technique employed to obtain
\widehat{f}(\mathbf{x})
.
By repeating this bootstrap procedure multiple times, we can construct empirical confidence intervals for the target regression function
f_{0}(\mathbf{x})
. Under certain conditions, we establish the convergence property of our deep bootstrap method and provide coverage guarantees for the resulting confidence interval.

1.1
Contributions

Our contributions are shown as follows:

•

We propose a novel deep bootstrap method for nonparametric regression by introducing conditional diffusion models. Unlike conventional bootstrap approaches, which separately address resampling and nonparametric estimation, our method uses conditional diffusion models to jointly generate samples via learned stochastic dynamics while simultaneously conducting nonparametric estimation. This integrated approach effectively mitigates the challenges inherent in distribution estimation and sampling, particularly in high-dimensional or multimodal settings, thus significantly enhancing the applicability of bootstrap methods. Extensive numerical experiments validate the accuracy, efficiency, and scalability of our method across diverse regression scenarios.

•

We establish rigorous theoretical guarantees for the proposed method. In particular, we derive a sharp end-to-end convergence rate for the conditional diffusion model in Wasserstein distance.
This result not only affirms the validity of our method but also offers broader contributions to the theoretical understanding of diffusion models.
Building on this result, we further establish convergence guarantees for the resulting bootstrap procedure, ensuring the robustness and reliability of the inference.

1.2
Related Work

In this section, we review related works with a primary focus on bootstrap and diffusion models.

Bootstrap.

In statistics, the Bootstrap is a fundamental tool for assessing uncertainty and conducting inference in a data-driven manner, and it has been widely adopted across various domains.
Bootstrap methods provide important statistical inference techniques for nonparametric regression
(
Efron and Tibshirani 1994
;
Shao and Tu 2012
;
Hall 2013
)
. Next, we review several key Bootstrap approaches in this context.
In nonparametric regression, the objective is to estimate the conditional mean function
f_{0}(\mathbf{x})=\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{x}]
. Estimators such as kernel,
splines, wavelets,
local polynomial regressions, nearest-neighbor regression models
(
Györfi et al. 2002
;
Wasserman 2006
;
Biau and Devroye 2015
)

and DNNs
(
Bauer and Kohler 2019
;
Schmidt-Hieber 2020
;
Kohler and Langer 2021
;
Farrell et al. 2021
;
Jiao et al. 2023
;
Bhattacharya et al. 2024
)
offer flexibility for modeling nonlinear relationships.
Besides the nonparametric estimator, to address the challenges of statistical inference, a variety of Bootstrap methods have been developed for nonparametric regression, resulting in the emergence of diverse resampling techniques.
One of the earliest approaches is the residual Bootstrap
(
Freedman 1981
)
, which involves computing residuals from a fitted nonparametric estimator
\widehat{f}(\mathbf{x})
, resampling these residuals (
\hat{\epsilon}_{i}:=\mathbf{Y}_{i}-\widehat{f}(\mathbf{X}_{i})
), and adding them back to the fitted values:

\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{*}=\widehat{f}(\mathbf{X}_{i})+\epsilon_{i}^{*},\quad\epsilon_{i}^{*}\sim\{\hat{\epsilon}_{1},\dots,\hat{\epsilon}_{n}\}.

In the wild Bootstrap
(
Härdle and Mammen 1993
;
Härdle and Marron 1991
)
, it modifies the residuals with random weights to preserve conditional variance:

\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{*}=\widehat{f}(\mathbf{X}_{i})+\hat{\epsilon}_{i}\cdot\xi_{i},

where
\xi_{i}
are i.i.d. random variables with mean zero and unit variance (e.g., Rademacher or standard normal). The wild Bootstrap is more adaptable in the presence of non-constant variance but still assumes a reliable estimate of
\widehat{f}(\mathbf{x})
and its residuals.
The paired Bootstrap takes a different route by naive resampling original observations
\{(\mathbf{X}_{i},\mathbf{Y}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
. This procedure implicitly retains the joint distribution of
(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})
and avoids estimating residuals altogether. However, it may perform poorly when the marginal distribution of
\mathbf{X}
is not of primary inferential interest, or when the structure of
P_{\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X}}
is more nuanced.
To better approximate the covariate distribution and cover sparse regions in
\mathbf{X}
, the smooth Bootstrap
(
Silverman and Young 1987
)
was developed. This method augments the paired Bootstrap by perturbing the covariates using a smooth kernel density estimate, generating
\mathbf{X}_{i}^{*}
by sampling from the estimated density.
Beyond these strategies, conditional Bootstrap methods are proposed for settings where a reliable estimate of the conditional distribution
P_{\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X}}
is obtainable. In this framework, one first obtain an estimator
\widehat{P}_{\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X}}
of
P_{\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X}}
using a suitable statistical or machine learning model such as kernel density estimators, then generates new responses
\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{*}\sim\widehat{P}_{\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{X}_{i}}

(
Rutherford and Yakowitz 1991
)
. This technique accommodates heteroscedasticity and complex conditional relationships, provided that
\widehat{P}_{\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X}}
is accurate.
As data becomes
higher-dimensional and more complex, traditional bootstrap methods encounter practical limitations, including inefficiencies in sparse settings, inability to capture multimodal behavior, and degraded performance in high dimensions. To address these issues, we propose a deep bootstrap method by incorporating conditional diffusion models to directly model
P_{\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X}}
, synthesize new response variables, and construct the nonparametric estimator of the underlying regression function. Conditional diffusion models flexibly capture complex relationships between covariates and responses, including multimodal and high-dimensional distributions, making them particularly effective in challenging high-dimensional regression settings.

Diffusion Models.

Diffusion models
(
Sohl-Dickstein et al. 2015
;
Song and Ermon 2019
;
Ho et al. 2020
;
Song and Ermon 2020
;
Song et al. 2020
;
Nichol and Dhariwal 2021
;
Yang et al. 2023
;
Chen et al. 2024
)
have recently emerged as a powerful and versatile class of generative models, establishing themselves as a central component of modern generative artificial intelligence. These models have demonstrated state-of-the-art performance across a broad spectrum of applications, including image synthesis, text-to-image generation, and scientific domains
(
Dhariwal and Nichol 2021
;
Ho et al. 2022
;
Rombach et al. 2022
;
Saharia et al. 2022
;
Zhang et al. 2023
;
Han et al. 2022
;
Li et al. 2022
)
. Their ability to generate high-fidelity and diverse samples with strong theoretical guarantees has positioned diffusion models as a leading framework in the landscape of deep generative modeling.
Diffusion models are fundamentally grounded in stochastic differential equations (SDEs). This mathematical formulation enables them to model complex, high-dimensional data distributions in a probabilistic framework.
Generally, diffusion models consist of two key stages: the forward process and the backward process.
The forward process defines a SDE that progressively perturbs the input data by injecting Gaussian noise over a continuous time horizon. This transformation gradually maps the data distribution into a simple and tractable prior, typically a standard Gaussian.
Conversely, the backward process is characterized by a reverse-time SDE that aims to denoise samples drawn from the prior distribution. By reversing the diffusion trajectory, this process reconstructs samples that approximate the original data distribution.

Formally, the forward process is described by an Itô SDE:

\displaystyle d\mathbf{x}^{F}_{t}=f(t,\mathbf{x}^{F}_{t})\,dt+g(t)\,d\mathbf{w}_{t},\quad\mathbf{x}^{F}_{0}\sim p_{\text{data}}(\mathbf{x}),

(1)

where
\mathbf{x}^{F}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathcal{X}}}
denotes the state at time
t\in[0,T]
,
\mathbf{w}_{t}
is a standard
d_{\mathcal{X}}
-dimensional Brown motion,
and
p_{\text{data}}(\mathbf{x})
is the target distribution. In the context of conditional diffusion models, this target corresponds to a conditional distribution.
The drift term
f:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathcal{X}}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathcal{X}}}
and diffusion coefficient
g:[0,T]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
govern the deterministic and stochastic components of the system, respectively.
This forward process progressively corrupts the original data by adding Gaussian noise, eventually mapping the data distribution
p_{\text{data}}
to a tractable prior distribution (e.g., a standard Gaussian) as
t\rightarrow\infty
.
As studied in
Anderson 1982
;
Haussmann and Pardoux 1986
, the corresponding reverse-time process is also governed by an SDE. Specifically, the reverse-time SDE takes the form:

\displaystyle d\mathbf{x}^{R}_{t}=\left[f(t,\mathbf{x}^{R}_{t})-g(t)^{2}\nabla_{\mathbf{x}^{R}_{t}}\log p_{t}(\mathbf{x}^{R}_{t})\right]dt+g(t)\,d\mathbf{w}^{R}_{t},

(2)

where
\mathbf{w}^{R}_{t}
is a standard Brown motion run backward in time,

p_{t}
denotes the marginal distribution of
\mathbf{x}^{R}_{t}
.
and
\nabla_{\mathbf{x}^{R}_{t}}\log p_{t}(\mathbf{x}^{R}_{t})
refers to the score function of
p_{t}
.
Let
t\rightarrow T-t
,
we can reformulate the reverse-time SDE (
2
) as a
forward-time equation:

\displaystyle d\mathbf{y}_{t}=\left[g^{2}(T-t)\nabla\log p_{T-t}(\mathbf{y}_{t})-f(T-t,\mathbf{y}_{t})\right]dt+g(T-t)d\mathbf{w}_{t},\penalty\ t\in[0,T],

(3)

where
\mathbf{y}_{t}=\mathbf{x}_{T-t}
.
We note that the SDEs in (
1
)–(
3
) are defined over an infinite time horizon as
T\to\infty
, which poses practical challenges for computation when applying numerical solvers.
To address this, one can apply an exponential time reparameterization
t\mapsto\exp(-t)

(
Albergo et al. 2023
)
, which effectively maps the infinite horizon onto a compact interval
[0,1]
.
In this work, we adopt
variance-preserving (VP) conditional diffusion models that are defined directly over the unit interval
t\in[0,1]
, in contrast to the original formulation in
(
Song et al. 2020
)
, which operates over an infinite time horizon.
Furthermore, in diffusion models, the score function
\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\log p_{t}(\mathbf{x})
is typically approximated using DNNs. This approximation enables practical sampling by numerically solving the corresponding SDE with the learned score network.
This score estimation is not only critical for generating high-quality samples but also plays a foundational role in the theoretical analysis of diffusion models. Existing theoretical work can be broadly classified into two categories based on how the score estimation error is handled.
The first category assumes a well-controlled approximation of the score function and derives guarantees under this assumption
(
Chen et al. 2023a
;
Conforti et al. 2023
;
Lee et al. 2022
;
Lee et al. 2023
;
Benton et al. 2023
;
Li et al. 2023
;
Gao et al. 2023
)
. The second line of research avoids assuming access to accurate scores and instead conducts end-to-end error analysis, directly bounding the discrepancy between the generated and true data distributions without requiring precise control of the score error
(
Oko et al. 2023
;
Chen et al. 2023b
;
Jiao et al. 2024
;
Fu et al. 2024
;
Jiao et al. 2025
)
.
Among these works,
Chen et al. 2023b
;
Jiao et al. 2025
assume that the score function is Lipschitz continuous and design
Lipschitz-continuous neural networks to approximate it. However, this technique leads to suboptimal convergence rates.
To address this limitation,
Oko et al. 2023
;
Jiao et al. 2024
;
Fu et al. 2024
consider a more general setting where the score function is Hölder continuous. They develop a more refined approximation technique by separately approximating the numerator and denominator of the score expression, ultimately deriving sharp convergence rates.

1.3
Preliminary

Notations.

We introduce the notations used throughout this paper.
Let
[\mathcal{M}]:=\{0,1,\cdots,\mathcal{M}\}
represent the set of integers ranging from 0 to
\mathcal{M}
.
Let
\mathbb{N}^{+}
denote the set of positive integers.
d_{\mathcal{X}},d_{\mathcal{Y}}
denote the dimension of
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}
or
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}
.
For matrices
A,B\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, we assert
A\preccurlyeq B
when the matrix
B-A
is positive semi-definite. The identity matrix in
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is denoted as
\mathbf{I}_{d}
.
The
\ell^{2}
-norm of a vector
\mathbf{x}=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{d}\}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is defined by
\|\mathbf{x}\|:=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}^{2}}
.
The
L^{\infty}(K)
-norm, denoted as

\|{f}\|_{L^{\infty}(K)}:=\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in K}|f(\mathbf{x})|
,
captures the supremum of the absolute values of a function over a set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For a vector function
\mathbf{v}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the
L^{\infty}(K)
-norm is defined as
\|\mathbf{v}\|_{L^{\infty}(K)}:=\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in K}\|\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{x})\|
. The asymptotic notation
f(\mathbf{x})=\mathcal{O}\left(g(\mathbf{x})\right)
is employed to signify that
f(\mathbf{x})\leq Cg(\mathbf{x})
for some constant
C>0
. Additionally, the notation
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\cdot)
is utilized to discount logarithmic factors in the asymptotic analysis.

Definition 1.1

(ReLU DNNs)
.

A class of deep neural networks NN
(L,M,J,\kappa)
with depth
L
, width
M
,
sparsity level
J
,
weight bound
\kappa
,
is defined as

\displaystyle{\rm{\penalty\penalty\penalty\penalty\displaystyle NN}}(L,M,J,\kappa)

\displaystyle=\Big\{\mathbf{b}(t,\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}):=(\mathbf{W}_{L}{\rm{ReLU}}(\cdot)+\mathbf{b}_{L})\circ\cdots\circ(\mathbf{W}_{2}{\rm{ReLU}}(\cdot)+\mathbf{b}_{2})\circ(\mathbf{W}_{1}(\cdot)+\mathbf{b}_{1})([t,\mathbf{y}^{\top},\mathbf{x}^{\top}]^{\top}):

\displaystyle\penalty\penalty\penalty\penalty\penalty\penalty\penalty\penalty\penalty\penalty\penalty\penalty\penalty\penalty\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{i+1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{i+1}\times d_{i}},\mathbf{b}_{i+1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{i+1}},i=0,1,\ldots,L-1,M:=\max\{d_{0},\ldots,d_{L}\},

\displaystyle\penalty\penalty\penalty\penalty\penalty\penalty\penalty\penalty\penalty\penalty\penalty\penalty\penalty\penalty\displaystyle\mathop{\max}_{1\leq{i}\leq{L}}\{\|{\mathbf{b}}_{i}\|_{\infty},\|{\mathbf{W}_{i}}\|_{\infty}\}\leq{\kappa},\penalty\ \sum_{i=1}^{L}\left(\|{\mathbf{W}_{i}}\|_{0}+\|{\mathbf{b}_{i}}\|_{0}\right)\leq J\Big\}.

Definition 1.2

(Wasserstein distance)
.

Let
\mu
and
\nu
be two probability measures defined on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with finite second moments, the
second-order Wasserstein distance is defined as:

\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu,\nu):=\left(\inf_{\gamma\in\mathcal{D}(\mu,\nu)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|^{2}\,\gamma(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x},\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})\right)^{1/2},

where
\mathcal{D}(\mu,\nu)
denotes the set of probability measures
\gamma
on
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
such that their respective marginal distributions are
\mu
and
\nu
.

Definition 1.3

(Covering number)
.

Let
\rho
be a pseudo-metric on
\mathcal{U}
and
S\subseteq\mathcal{U}
. For any
\delta>0
, a set
A\subseteq\mathcal{U}
is called a
\delta
-covering of
S
if for any
\mathbf{x}\in S
there exists
\mathbf{y}\in A
such that
\rho(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\leq\delta
. The
\delta
-covering number of
S
, denoted by
\mathcal{N}(\delta,S,\rho)
, is the minimum cardinality of any
\delta
-covering of
S
.

Definition 1.4

((
\beta
,
R
)-Hölder Class)
.

Let
\beta=r+s>0
be a degree of smoothness, where

r
is an integer and
s\in(0,1]
. For a function
f:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, its Hölder norm is defined as

\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\beta}}:=\max_{\|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\|_{1}\leq r}\|\partial^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}f\|_{\infty}+\max_{\|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\|_{1}=r}\sup_{\mathbf{x}\neq\mathbf{y}}\frac{|\partial^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}f(\mathbf{x})-\partial^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}f(\mathbf{y})|}{\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|^{s}},

where
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
is a multi-index. We say a function
f
is
\beta
-Hölder, if and only if
\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\beta}}<\infty
. The (
\beta
,
R
)-Hölder class for some constant
R>0
is defined as

\mathcal{H}^{\beta}(\Omega,R):=\Big\{f:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\Big|\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\beta}}\leq R\Big\}.

1.4
Outlines

The remainder of this paper is organized as follows.
In Section
2
, we present our proposed deep bootstrap method.
The theoretical analysis of our proposed method is presented in Section
3
.
In Section

4
, we give the numerical experiments.
We conclude in Section
5
.
In the Appendix, we provide detailed proofs for all lemmas and theorems.

2
Method

In this section, we introduce our proposed deep bootstrap method for nonparametric regression. The first step, presented in Section
2.1
, involves constructing a conditional diffusion model.
Using this model, we can estimate the target conditional distribution, and further perform nonparametric regression by computing the sample mean of the generated data. Subsequently, we generate bootstrap samples through the conditional diffusion model to formulate a bootstrap procedure for statistical inference, as detailed in Section
2.2
.

2.1
Conditional Diffusion Model

In this section, we formulate the VP conditional diffusion model over the unit time interval

t\in[0,1]
to learn the conditional distribution.
It is important to emphasize that the model is developed for a general setting in which the variable
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}}
is a

d_{\mathcal{Y}}
-dimensional vector.

Diffusion Process.
The forward diffusion process is defined as:

\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}_{t}^{F}=-\frac{\mathbf{y}^{F}_{t}}{1-t}\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\frac{2}{1-t}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{w}_{t},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{y}_{0}^{F}\sim p_{0}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x}).

(4)

It determines a diffusion process that starts with conditional distribution
p_{0}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})
at time
t=0
and evolves toward the standard Gaussian distribution
p_{1}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{d_{\mathcal{Y}}})
at time
t=1
. Conditioning on some observation
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
, the transition probability distribution from
\mathbf{y}_{0}^{F}
to
\mathbf{y}_{t}^{F}
is given by
\mathbf{y}_{t}^{F}|\mathbf{y}_{0}^{F}\sim\mathcal{N}(m_{t}\mathbf{y}_{0}^{F},\sigma_{t}^{2}\mathbf{I}_{d_{\mathcal{Y}}})
, where
m_{t}:=1-t
,
\sigma_{t}:=\sqrt{t(2-t)}
. The process describes how data are transformed into noises during unit-time
interval.

The SDE above can be reversed if we know the score of the
distribution at each intermediate time step,
\nabla\log p_{t}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})
. The reverse SDE reads

\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}_{t}^{R}=\left[-\frac{\mathbf{y}_{t}^{R}}{1-t}-\frac{2}{1-t}\nabla\log p_{t}(\mathbf{y}_{t}^{R}|\mathbf{x})\right]\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\frac{2}{1-t}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{w}_{t}^{R},

(5)

where
t
flows from 1 to 0 and
\mathbf{w}_{t}^{R}
is a time reverse Brownian motion.
For convenience, we take transformation
t\rightarrow 1-t
, and then we can rewrite (
5
) as a time forward version:

\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}_{t}=\left[\frac{\mathbf{y}_{t}}{t}+\frac{2}{t}\nabla\log p_{1-t}(\mathbf{y}_{t}|\mathbf{x})\right]\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\frac{2}{t}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{w}_{t},\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{y}_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbf{I}_{d_{\mathcal{Y}}}),\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathbf{y}_{1}\sim p_{0}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x}).

(6)

It is obvious that
\mathbf{y}_{t}\sim p_{1-t}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})
.

Score Matching.

We apply score matching techniques
(
Hyvärinen and Dayan 2005
;
Vincent 2011
)
to estimate the target conditional score function of SDE (
6
), defined as

\mathbf{b}^{*}(t,\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}):=\nabla\log{p}_{t}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})
.
It can be verified that
\mathbf{b}^{*}
minimizes the loss function
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{b})
over all measurable functions, where

\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{b}):=\frac{1}{1-2T}\int_{T}^{1-T}\frac{1}{1-t}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{F})}\|{\mathbf{b}(t,\mathbf{y}_{t}^{F},\mathbf{x})-\nabla\log{p_{t}(\mathbf{y}_{t}^{F}|\mathbf{x})}}\|^{2}\mathrm{d}t,

with
0<T<1
.
We notice that the deliberate selection of
T
is motivated by the necessity to preclude the score function from exhibiting a blow-up at
T=0
, concomitantly facilitating the stabilization of the training process for the model.
This time truncation strategy is not unique to our work; it has also been used in both the training and theoretical analysis of diffusion models

(
Song and Ermon 2020
;
Vahdat et al. 2021
;
Chen et al. 2023b
;
Chen et al. 2023a
;
Oko et al. 2023
)
.
By
Hyvärinen and Dayan 2005
;
Vincent 2011
, we alternatively adopt a denoising score matching objective. With a slight abuse of notation, we denote this objective as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{b})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-2T}\int_{T}^{1-T}\frac{1}{1-t}\left(\mathbb{E}_{(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{0}^{F})}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{y}_{t}^{F}\mid(\mathbf{y}_{0}^{F},\mathbf{x})}\left\|\mathbf{b}(t,\mathbf{y}_{t}^{F},\mathbf{x})+\frac{\mathbf{y}_{t}^{F}-m_{t}\mathbf{y}_{0}^{F}}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\right\|^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-2T}\int_{T}^{1-T}\frac{1}{1-t}\left(\mathbb{E}_{(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{0}^{F})}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{z};\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{d_{\mathcal{Y}}})}\left\|\mathbf{b}(t,m_{t}\mathbf{y}_{0}^{F}+\sigma_{t}\mathbf{z},\mathbf{x})+\frac{\mathbf{z}}{\sigma_{t}}\right\|^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}t.

Given
n
i.i.d. samples

\{(\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{y}_{0,i}^{F})\}_{i=1}^{n}
from
p(\mathbf{x})p_{0}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})
,
and
m
i.i.d. samples
\{(t_{j},\mathbf{z}_{j})\}_{j=1}^{m}
from
\mathrm{U}[T,1-T]
and
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{z};\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{d_{\mathcal{Y}}})
, we can employ the empirical risk minimizer (ERM) to estimate the conditional score function
\mathbf{b}^{*}
. This ERM, denoted by
\widehat{\mathbf{b}}
, is obtained by

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{b}}\in{\mathop{\rm{argmin}}_{\mathbf{b}\in\mathrm{NN}}}\penalty\ \widehat{\mathcal{L}}(\mathbf{b}):=\frac{1}{mn}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{1-t_{j}}\left\|\mathbf{b}(t_{j},m_{t_{j}}\mathbf{y}_{0,i}^{F}+\sigma_{t_{j}}\mathbf{z}_{j},\mathbf{x}_{i})+\frac{\mathbf{z}_{j}}{\sigma_{t_{j}}}\right\|^{2},

(7)

where
NN
refers to ReLU DNNs defined in
Definition
1.1
.

Exponential Integrator Discretization.

Given the estimated score function
\widehat{\mathbf{b}}
, as defined in (
7
), we can formulate an SDE initializing from the prior distribution:

\mathrm{d}\widehat{\mathbf{y}}_{t}=\left[\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{y}}_{t}}{t}+\frac{2\widehat{\mathbf{b}}(1-t,\widehat{\mathbf{y}}_{t},\mathbf{x})}{t}\right]\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\frac{2}{t}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{w}_{t},\penalty\ \widehat{\mathbf{y}}_{0}=\mathbf{y}_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbf{I}_{d_{\mathcal{Y}}}).

(8)

Now, we employ a discrete-time approximation for the sampling dynamics (
8
).
Let

T=t_{0}<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{K}=1-T,\penalty\ K\in\mathbb{N}^{+},

be the discretization points on
[T,1-T]
and satisfy
\frac{t_{i+1}}{t_{i}}\leq 2
,
0\leq i\leq K-1
. We consider the explicit exponential integrator scheme:

\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}_{t}=\left[\frac{\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}_{t_{i}}}{t_{i}}+\frac{2\widehat{\mathbf{b}}(1-t_{i},\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}_{t_{i}},\mathbf{x})}{t_{i}}\right]\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\frac{2}{t}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{w}_{t},\penalty\ \widetilde{\mathbf{y}}_{t_{0}}=\mathbf{y}_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{d_{\mathcal{Y}}}),\penalty\ t\in[t_{i},t_{i+1}),

(9)

for
i=0,1,\cdots,K-1
.
Subsequently, we can utilize dynamics (
9
) to generate new samples.
The proposed conditional diffusion model is summarized in the following algorithm.

Algorithm 1

The Proposed Conditional Diffusion Model

1.
Input:

T
,
K
,
\{(\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{y}_{0,i}^{F})\}_{i=1}^{n}\sim p(\mathbf{x})p_{0}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})
,

\{(t_{j},\mathbf{z}_{j})\}_{j=1}^{m}\sim\mathrm{U}[T,1-T]
and
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{z};\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{d_{\mathcal{Y}}})

2.
Score estimation:

Obtain
\widehat{\mathbf{b}}
by (
7
).

3.
Sampling procedure:

Sample

\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}_{0}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{d_{\mathcal{Y}}})
;

for all

i=0,1,\ldots,K-1

do

Sample
\mathbf{\epsilon}_{t_{i}}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{d_{\mathcal{Y}}})
;

\mathbf{b}(\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}_{t_{i}})=\frac{\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}_{t_{i}}}{t_{i}}+\frac{2\widehat{\mathbf{b}}(1-t_{i},\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}_{t_{i}},\mathbf{x})}{t_{i}}
;

\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}_{t_{i+1}}=\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}_{t_{i}}+\frac{1-2T}{K}\mathbf{b}(\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}_{t_{i}})+\sqrt{2\ln(t_{i+1}/t_{i})}\mathbf{\epsilon}_{t_{i}}
;

end

for

4.
Output:

\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}_{t_{K}}
.

2.2
Bootstrap via Conditional Diffusion Model

In this section, we utilize the conditional diffusion model proposed in the previous section to construct our deep bootstrap method. Throughout this development, we focus exclusively on the univariate case where
d_{\mathcal{Y}}=1
. However, for notational consistency and to facilitate potential generalization, we may retain the use of
d_{\mathcal{Y}}
in contexts where no ambiguity arises. Given an i.i.d. dataset
\mathcal{D}=\{(\mathbf{X}_{i},\mathbf{Y}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
, we formulate a conditional diffusion model as described in Section
2.1
to learn the target conditional distribution
P_{\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X}}
, and denote the resulting distribution as
\widehat{P}_{\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X}}
. Using this model, we generate a synthetic dataset

\{\widehat{\mathbf{Y}}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(j)}\}_{j=1}^{n}\sim\widehat{P}_{\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{x}}
by running sampling dynamic
(
9
)
n
times, where the samples are approximately distributed according to
P_{\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{x}}
.
Recall that
\mathbf{Y}_{\mathbf{x}}\sim P_{\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{x}}
and the underlying regression function is
f_{0}(\mathbf{x})=\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{Y}_{\mathbf{x}})
.
We estimate the underlying regression function using the sample mean of the generated data:

\displaystyle\widehat{f}(\mathbf{x}):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\widehat{\mathbf{Y}}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(j)}.

(10)

Furthermore, given the original covariates
\{\mathbf{X}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
, we can construct a bootstrap dataset
\mathcal{D}^{*}:=\{\mathbf{X}_{i},\widehat{Y}_{\mathbf{X}_{i}}\}_{i=1}^{n}
by generating responses from the conditional diffusion model.
Based on this bootstrap dataset
\mathcal{D}^{*}
, we re-train a conditional diffusion model to learn an updated distribution
\widehat{P}^{*}_{\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X}}
, which is approximated to

\widehat{P}_{\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X}}
. Using
\widehat{P}^{*}_{\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X}}
, we generate dataset

\{\widehat{\mathbf{Y}}^{*,(j)}_{\mathbf{x}}\}_{j=1}^{n}\sim\widehat{P}^{*}_{\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{x}}
, and define the corresponding bootstrap estimator as

\widehat{f}^{*}(\mathbf{x}):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\widehat{\mathbf{Y}}^{*,(j)}_{\mathbf{x}}
.
The procedure can be summarized as follows:

(I)

Obtain
\widehat{f}(\mathbf{x})
:

\mathcal{D}=\{\mathbf{X}_{i},\mathbf{Y}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\rightarrow\mbox{
Conditional diffusion model (Algorithm \ref{sampling_algorithm})
}\rightarrow\{\widehat{\mathbf{Y}}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(j)}\}_{j=1}^{n}\sim\widehat{P}_{\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{x}}\rightarrow\widehat{f}(\mathbf{x}):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\widehat{\mathbf{Y}}_{\mathbf{x}}^{(j)};

(II)

Obtain
\widehat{f}^{*}(\mathbf{x})
:

\mathcal{D}^{*}:=\{\mathbf{X}_{i},\widehat{\mathbf{Y}}_{\mathbf{X}_{i}}\}_{i=1}^{n}\rightarrow\mbox{
Conditional diffusion model (Algorithm \ref{sampling_algorithm})
}\rightarrow\{\widehat{\mathbf{Y}}^{*,(j)}_{\mathbf{x}}\}_{j=1}^{n}\sim\widehat{P}^{*}_{\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{x}}\rightarrow\widehat{f}^{*}(\mathbf{x}):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\widehat{\mathbf{Y}}^{*,(j)}_{\mathbf{x}}
.

Consequently, by repeating the above Step (
II
)
\mathcal{B}
(
\mathcal{B}>1
) times, we can construct a confidence interval for the underlying regression function
f_{0}(\mathbf{x})
.
Let
\widehat{f}_{b}^{*}(\mathbf{x})
denote the
b
-th bootstrap estimator of
\widehat{f}(\mathbf{x})
, for
b\in\{1,\ldots,\mathcal{B}\}
. We define the centered statistic as

\widehat{R}_{b}^{*}(\mathbf{x}):=\widehat{f}_{b}^{*}(\mathbf{x})-\widehat{f}(\mathbf{x}),

and let
\widehat{H}^{*}(r)
,
r\in\mathbb{R}
, denote its empirical cumulative distribution function (CDF):

\widehat{H}^{*}(r):=\frac{1}{\mathcal{B}}\sum_{b=1}^{\mathcal{B}}\mathbf{I}\left(\widehat{R}_{b}^{*}(\mathbf{x})\leq r\right),

where
\mathbf{I}(\cdot)
denotes the indicator function.
Then, the
(1-\alpha)
(
\alpha\in(0,1)
)
confidence interval for
f_{0}(\mathbf{x})
is given by

\displaystyle\left[\widehat{f}(\mathbf{x})-(\widehat{H}^{*})^{-1}(1-\alpha/2),\;\widehat{f}(\mathbf{x})-(\widehat{H}^{*})^{-1}(\alpha/2)\right],

(11)

where
(\widehat{H}^{*})^{-1}(\cdot)
denotes the quantile function (i.e., the inverse CDF) of
\widehat{H}^{*}(\cdot)
.

In summary, our proposed deep bootstrap method is outlined in the following algorithm.

Algorithm 2

Bootstrap via Conditional Diffusion Models

1.
Input:

\mathcal{B}
,
T
,
K
,
\{(\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{y}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}\sim P_{\mathbf{X}}\times P_{\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X}}
,

\{(t_{j},\mathbf{z}_{j})\}_{j=1}^{m}\sim\mathrm{U}[T,1-T]
and
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{z};\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{d_{\mathcal{Y}}})

2.
Obtain the nonparametric estimator
\widehat{f}(\mathbf{x})
by (
I
).

3.
Obtain Bootstrap estimators

\{\widehat{f}_{b}^{*}(\mathbf{x})\}_{b=1}^{\mathcal{B}}
:

for

b=1,\ldots,\mathcal{B}

do

Compute
\widehat{f}_{b}^{*}(\mathbf{x})
by using Step (
II
)

end

for

4.
Output:
The asymptotic
1-\alpha
confidence interval (
11
).

3
Theory

In this section, we establish the theoretical foundations of the proposed method. Section
3.1
analyzes the conditional diffusion model presented in Algorithm
1
and derives a sharp convergence rate in Wasserstein distance. Building on this result, Section
3.2
provides a convergence analysis of the deep bootstrap procedure described in Algorithm
2
.

3.1
Convergence of Conditional Diffusion Model

In this section, we obtain the convergence rate for the conditional diffusion model. We denote
p_{1-t}(\cdot|\mathbf{x})
,
\widehat{p}_{1-t}(\cdot|\mathbf{x})
, and
\widetilde{p}_{1-t}(\cdot|\mathbf{x})
as the distributions of
\mathbf{y}_{t}
,
\widehat{\mathbf{y}}_{t}
, and
\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}_{t}
correspondingly.
Since the diffusion process (
6
) converges to
p_{0}(\cdot|\mathbf{x})
as
t\rightarrow 1
and the domain is compact, we define a truncated estimator
p_{T}^{B}(\cdot|\mathbf{x})
for
p_{0}(\cdot|\mathbf{x})
as the distribution of
\mathbf{y}_{1-T}\cdot\mathbf{I}_{\{\|\mathbf{y}_{1-T}\|_{\infty}\leq B\}}
, for a large constant
B>0
such that
p_{T}^{B}(\cdot|\mathbf{x})\approx p_{0}(\cdot|\mathbf{x})
.
Similarly, we define
\widehat{p}_{T}^{B}(\cdot|\mathbf{x})
and
\widetilde{p}_{T}^{B}(\cdot|\mathbf{x})
as the distributions of
\widehat{\mathbf{y}}_{1-T}\cdot\mathbf{I}_{\{\|\widehat{\mathbf{y}}_{1-T}\|_{\infty}\leq B\}}
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}_{1-T}\cdot\mathbf{I}_{\{\|\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}_{1-T}\|_{\infty}\leq B\}}
, respectively. Here, we truncate the supports of the distributions
p_{T}(\cdot|\mathbf{x})
,
\widehat{p}_{T}(\cdot|\mathbf{x})
,
and
\widetilde{p}_{T}(\cdot|\mathbf{x})
to ensure a bounded support for the distribution estimators, which is done for technical convenience in the theoretical analysis.

Let
\mathcal{D}:=\{(\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{y}_{0,i}^{F})\}_{i=1}^{n}
,
\mathcal{T}:=\{t_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}
, and
\mathcal{Z}:=\{\mathbf{z}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}
. We define

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbf{b}):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell_{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{y}_{0,i}^{F}),\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{Z}}(\mathbf{b}):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\ell}_{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{y}_{0,i}^{F}),

where

\ell_{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{0}^{F}):=\frac{1}{1-2T}\int_{T}^{1-T}\frac{1}{1-t}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{z}}\left\|\mathbf{b}(t,m_{t}\mathbf{y}_{0}^{F}+\sigma_{t}\mathbf{z},\mathbf{x})+\frac{\mathbf{z}}{\sigma_{t}}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}t,

and

\widehat{\ell}_{\mathbf{b}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{0}^{F}):=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{1}{1-t_{j}}\left\|\mathbf{b}(t_{j},m_{t_{j}}\mathbf{y}_{0}^{F}+\sigma_{t_{j}}\mathbf{z}_{j},\mathbf{x})+\frac{\mathbf{z}_{j}}{\sigma_{t_{j}}}\right\|^{2}.

Then, for any
\mathbf{b}\in\mathrm{NN}
, it yields that

\displaystyle\penalty\penalty\penalty\penalty\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\mathbf{b}})-\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{b}^{*})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\mathbf{b}})-2\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{D}}(\widehat{\mathbf{b}})+\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{b}^{*})+2\left(\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{D}}(\widehat{\mathbf{b}})-\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{b}^{*})\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\mathbf{b}})-2\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{D}}(\widehat{\mathbf{b}})+\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{b}^{*})+2\left(\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{D}}(\widehat{\mathbf{b}})-\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{Z}}(\widehat{\mathbf{b}})\right)+2\left(\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{Z}}(\widehat{\mathbf{b}})-\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{b}^{*})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\mathbf{b}})-2\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{D}}(\widehat{\mathbf{b}})+\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{b}^{*})+2\left(\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{D}}(\widehat{\mathbf{b}})-\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{Z}}(\widehat{\mathbf{b}})\right)+2\left(\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{Z}}(\mathbf{b})-\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{b}^{*})\right).

Taking expectations, followed by taking the infimum over
\mathbf{b}\in\mathrm{NN}
on both sides of the above inequality, it holds that

\displaystyle\penalty\penalty\penalty\penalty\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{Z}}\left(\frac{1}{1-2T}\int_{T}^{1-T}\frac{1}{1-t}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{F})}\left\|{\widehat{\mathbf{b}}(t,\mathbf{y}_{t}^{F},\mathbf{x})-\nabla\log{p_{t}(\mathbf{y}_{t}^{F}|\mathbf{x})}}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{Z}}\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\mathbf{b}})-\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{b}^{*})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{Z}}\left(\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\mathbf{b}})-2\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{D}}(\widehat{\mathbf{b}})+\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{b}^{*})\right)+2\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{Z}}\left(\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{D}}(\widehat{\mathbf{b}})-\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{Z}}(\widehat{\mathbf{b}})\right)

\displaystyle\penalty\penalty\penalty\penalty\displaystyle+2\mathop{\mathrm{inf}}_{\mathbf{b}\in{\mathrm{NN}}}(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{b})-\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{b}^{*})).

In the above inequality,
the terms

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{Z}}\left(\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\mathbf{b}})-2\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{D}}(\widehat{\mathbf{b}})+\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{b}^{*})\right)+2\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{Z}}\left(\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{D}}(\widehat{\mathbf{b}})-\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{Z}}(\widehat{\mathbf{b}})\right)

and

\mathop{\mathrm{inf}}_{\mathbf{b}\in{\mathrm{NN}}}(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{b})-\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{b}^{*}))

denote the statistical error and approximation error, respectively.

Using tools from empirical process theory and deep approximation theory, we derive upper bounds for the associated errors. To proceed, we first introduce the following necessary assumptions.

Assumption 3.1

(Bounded Conditional Density)
.

Suppose that the target conditional density
p_{0}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})
is supported on
[-1,1]^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}}\times[-1,1]^{d_{\mathcal{X}}}
and is bounded above and below by two constants
C_{u}
and
C_{l}
, respectively.

Assumption 3.2

(Hölder Continuity)
.

The conditional density
p_{0}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})\in\mathcal{H}^{\beta}([-1,1]^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}}\times[-1,1]^{d_{\mathcal{X}}},R)
for a Hölder index
\beta>0
and a constant
R>0
.

Assumption 3.3

(Boundary Smoothness)
.

There exists a constant
0<a<1
such that
p_{0}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}([-1,1]^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}}\backslash[-1+a,1-a]^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}}\times[-1,1]^{d_{\mathcal{X}}})
.

Assumption 3.4

(Bounded derivative)
.

For any
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{N}^{d_{\mathcal{X}}}
, the partial derivative of
p_{0}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})
with respect to
\mathbf{x}
,
\partial_{\mathbf{x}}^{\mathbf{u}}p_{0}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})
, is bounded by a constant
C_{\mathcal{X}}>0
on
[-1,1]^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}}\times[-1,1]^{d_{\mathcal{X}}}
, i.e.,
\left|\partial_{\mathbf{x}}^{\mathbf{u}}p_{0}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})\right|\leq C_{\mathcal{X}}
on
[-1,1]^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}}\times[-1,1]^{d_{\mathcal{X}}}
.

Remark 3.1
.

These foundational assumptions are critical to advancing the theoretical framework of diffusion models. In particular, the bounded support assumption has been extensively documented in prior studies, as exemplified by the works of
Lee et al. 2023
;
Li et al. 2023
;
Oko et al. 2023
. Similarly, within the field of nonparametric regression, imposing boundedness assumptions on the response variable is a common practice in the literature
Bauer and Kohler 2019
;
Györfi et al. 2002
;
Kohler and Langer 2021
;
Farrell et al. 2021
. This assumption carries substantial technical significance, and it holds potential for further generalization to unbounded scenarios through the inclusion of exponential tail properties. Assumption
3.2
enforces the Hölder continuity of the conditional density, a fundamental condition in nonparametric estimation that mirrors Assumption 3.1 in
Fu et al. 2024
. Furthermore, Assumption
3.3
and Assumption
3.4
establish two regularity conditions for the conditional density
p_{0}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})
, a technical assumption that also appears in
Oko et al. 2023
to support similar analytical goals.

Under Assumptions
3.1
-
3.4
,
we provide the following
lemma, which bounds the approximation error.

Lemma 3.1

(Approximation Error)
.

Suppose that Assumptions
3.1
-
3.4
hold. Let
\mathcal{M}\gg 1,C_{T}>0
, and
T=\mathcal{M}^{-C_{T}}
. Then we can choose a
ReLU neural network
\mathbf{b}\in\mathrm{NN}(L,M,J,\kappa)
that satisfies

\mathbf{b}(t,\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{b}(t,\mathbf{b}_{\mathrm{clip}}(\mathbf{y},-C_{0},C_{0}),\mathbf{x}),

for a constant
C_{0}=\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\log\mathcal{M}})
, and

\|\mathbf{b}(t,\cdot,\mathbf{x})\|_{\infty}\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{\log\mathcal{M}}}{\sigma_{t}},

and has the following structure:

L=\mathcal{O}(\log^{4}\mathcal{M}),M=\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{M}^{d_{\mathcal{X}}+d_{\mathcal{Y}}}\log^{7}\mathcal{M}),J=\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{M}^{d_{\mathcal{X}}+d_{\mathcal{Y}}}\log^{9}\mathcal{M}),\kappa=\exp\left(\mathcal{O}(\log^{4}\mathcal{M})\right).

Moreover, for any
t\in[\mathcal{M}^{-C_{T}},1-\mathcal{M}^{-C_{T}}]
and
\mathbf{x}\in[-1,1]^{d_{\mathcal{X}}}
, it holds that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}}}\|\mathbf{b}(t,\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x})-\mathbf{b}^{*}(t,\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x})\|^{2}p_{t}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}\lesssim\frac{\mathcal{M}^{-2\beta}\log\mathcal{M}}{\sigma_{t}^{2}},

where
\sigma_{t}=\sqrt{t(2-t)}
.

Now, we restrict the ReLU neural network class
\mathrm{NN}(L,M,J,\kappa)
to

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}:=\Big\{\mathbf{b}\in

\displaystyle\mathrm{NN}(L,M,J,\kappa)\Big|\|\mathbf{b}(t,\cdot,\mathbf{x})\|_{\infty}\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{\log\mathcal{M}}}{\sigma_{t}},

\displaystyle\mathbf{b}(t,\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{b}(t,\mathbf{b}_{\mathrm{clip}}(\mathbf{y},-C_{0},C_{0})\penalty\ \text{ for }\penalty\ \|\mathbf{y}\|_{\infty}>C_{0},\penalty\ \text{where}\penalty\ C_{0}=\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\log\mathcal{M}}),

\displaystyle L=\mathcal{O}(\log^{4}\mathcal{M}),M=\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{M}^{d_{\mathcal{X}}+d_{\mathcal{Y}}}\log^{7}\mathcal{M}),J=\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{M}^{d_{\mathcal{X}}+d_{\mathcal{Y}}}\log^{9}\mathcal{M}),\kappa=\exp\left(\mathcal{O}(\log^{4}\mathcal{M})\right)\Big\}.

Remark 3.2
.

The method for deriving the upper bound on the approximation error in Lemma
3.1
is inspired by
Oko et al. 2023
;
Fu et al. 2024
.
To elaborate, we express the conditional score function
\nabla\log p_{t}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})
as

\nabla\log p_{t}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})=\frac{\nabla p_{t}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})}{p_{t}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})}.

To obtain the approximation, we treat the numerator and denominator separately. Since the approximation techniques for both terms are analogous, we focus on approximating
p_{t}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})
as a representative example.
The first step involves truncating the integral domain in
p_{t}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})
. We denote

m_{t}:=1-t
,
\sigma_{t}:=\sqrt{t(2-t)}.
Recall that

p_{t}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}}}p_{0}(\mathbf{y}_{1}|\mathbf{x})\mathbf{I}_{\{\|\mathbf{y}_{1}\|_{\infty}\leq 1\}}\cdot\underbrace{\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}}(2\pi)^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|\mathbf{y}-m_{t}\mathbf{y}_{1}\|^{2}}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}\right)}_{\text{Transition Kernel}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}_{1}.

There exists a constant

C>0
such that for any
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}}
,

\left|p_{t}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})-\int_{A_{\mathbf{y}}}p_{0}(\mathbf{y}_{1}|\mathbf{x})\mathbf{I}_{\{\|\mathbf{y}_{1}\|_{\infty}\leq 1\}}\cdot\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}}(2\pi)^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|\mathbf{y}-m_{t}\mathbf{y}_{1}\|^{2}}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}\right)\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}_{1}\right|\lesssim\epsilon,

where
A_{\mathbf{y}}=\prod_{i=1}^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}}a_{i,\mathbf{y}}
with
a_{i,\mathbf{y}}=\left[\frac{y_{i}-C\sigma_{t}\sqrt{\log\epsilon^{-1}}}{m_{t}},\frac{y_{i}+C\sigma_{t}\sqrt{\log\epsilon^{-1}}}{m_{t}}\right]
.
Next, we approximate the integral over
A_{\mathbf{y}}
.
To do so, we introduce a Taylor polynomial
f_{\rm{Taylor}}(\mathbf{y}_{1},\mathbf{x})
to approximate
p_{0}(\mathbf{y}_{1}|\mathbf{x})
.
As a result,
p_{t}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})
is approximated as

\int_{A_{\mathbf{y}}}f_{\rm{Taylor}}(\mathbf{y}_{1},\mathbf{x})\mathbf{I}_{\{\|\mathbf{y}_{1}\|_{\infty}\leq 1\}}\cdot\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}}(2\pi)^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|\mathbf{y}-m_{t}\mathbf{y}_{1}\|^{2}}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}\right)\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}_{1}.

In the third step, we approximate the exponential transition kernel

\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}}(2\pi)^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|\mathbf{y}-m_{t}\mathbf{y}_{1}\|^{2}}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}\right)
using a Taylor polynomial denoted as
f_{\rm{Taylor}}^{\rm{kernel}}(t,\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}_{1})
.
This yields the approximation

\int_{A_{\mathbf{y}}}f_{\rm{Taylor}}(\mathbf{y}_{1},\mathbf{x})f_{\rm{Taylor}}^{\rm{kernel}}(t,\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}_{1})\mathbf{I}_{\{\|\mathbf{y}_{1}\|_{\infty}\leq 1\}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}_{1}.

Since the product
f_{\rm{Taylor}}(\mathbf{y}_{1},\mathbf{x})f_{\rm{Taylor}}^{\rm{kernel}}(t,\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}_{1})
is a polynomial, its integration can be evaluated explicitly.
In the fourth step, we utilize a DNN to approximate the resulting integral.
Integrating these analyses, we can ultimately construct a ReLU neural network that approximates the conditional score function.

Lemma 3.2

(Statistical Error)
.

Suppose that Assumptions
3.1
-
3.3
hold. Let
\mathcal{M}\gg 1
,
C_{T}>0
,
and
T=\mathcal{M}^{-C_{T}}
.
The score estimator
\widehat{\mathbf{b}}
defined in (
7
) with the neural network structures belonging to class
\mathcal{C}
, satisfies

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{Z}}\left(\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\mathbf{b}})-2\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{D}}(\widehat{\mathbf{b}})+\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{b}^{*})\right)\lesssim\frac{\mathcal{M}^{d_{\mathcal{X}}+d_{\mathcal{Y}}}\log^{15}\mathcal{M}\left(\log^{4}\mathcal{M}+\log n\right)}{n}

and

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{Z}}\left(\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{D}}(\widehat{\mathbf{b}})-\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{Z}}(\widehat{\mathbf{b}})\right)\lesssim\mathcal{M}^{C_{T}}(\log m+\log\mathcal{M})\cdot\frac{\mathcal{M}^{\frac{d_{\mathcal{X}}+d_{\mathcal{Y}}}{2}}\log^{\frac{13}{2}}\mathcal{M}(\log^{2}\mathcal{M}+\sqrt{\log m})}{\sqrt{m}}.

By combining the approximation and statistical errors presented in Lemmas
3.1
-
3.2
, we
can now derive the upper bound for the conditional score estimation, as detailed in the following theorem.

Theorem 3.3

(Error Bound for Conditional Score Estimation)
.

Suppose that Assumptions
3.1
-
3.4
hold.
By choosing
\mathcal{M}=\lfloor n^{\frac{1}{d_{\mathcal{X}}+d_{\mathcal{Y}}+2\beta}}\rfloor+1\lesssim n^{\frac{1}{d_{\mathcal{X}}+d_{\mathcal{Y}}+2\beta}}
,
m=n^{\frac{d_{\mathcal{X}}+d_{\mathcal{Y}}+8\beta}{d_{\mathcal{X}}+d_{\mathcal{Y}}+2\beta}}
,
and
C_{T}=2\beta
in
Lemmas
3.1
-
3.2
, the score estimator
\widehat{\mathbf{b}}
defined in (
7
)
with the neural network structure belonging to class
\mathcal{C}
, satisfies

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{Z}}\left(\frac{1}{1-2T}\int_{T}^{1-T}\frac{1}{t}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{y}_{t},\mathbf{x}}\|\widehat{\mathbf{b}}(1-t,\mathbf{y}_{t},\mathbf{x})-\nabla\log p_{1-t}(\mathbf{y}_{t}|\mathbf{x})\|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\right)\lesssim n^{-\frac{2\beta}{d_{\mathcal{X}}+d_{\mathcal{Y}}+2\beta}}\log^{19}n.

Now we consider to bound
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{Z}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\mathcal{W}_{2}(\widetilde{p}_{T}^{B}(\cdot|\mathbf{x}),p_{0}(\cdot|\mathbf{x}))]
. We systematically decompose this
error term into two distinct components, as outlined by the following inequality:

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{Z}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\mathcal{W}_{2}(\widetilde{p}_{T}^{B}(\cdot|\mathbf{x}),p_{0}(\cdot|\mathbf{x}))]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{Z}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\mathcal{W}_{2}(\widetilde{p}_{T}^{B}(\cdot|\mathbf{x}),p_{T}^{B}(\cdot|\mathbf{x}))]+\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\mathcal{W}_{2}(p_{T}^{B}(\cdot|\mathbf{x}),p_{0}(\cdot|\mathbf{x}))].

3.1.1
Bound
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{Z}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\mathcal{W}_{2}(\widetilde{p}_{T}^{B}(\cdot|\mathbf{x}),p_{T}^{B}(\cdot|\mathbf{x}))]

In this subsection, our aim is to establish an upper bound for
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{Z}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\mathcal{W}_{2}(\widetilde{p}_{T}^{B}(\cdot|\mathbf{x}),p_{T}^{B}(\cdot|\mathbf{x}))]
. With Theorem
3.3
, for any
\epsilon>0
, there exists
\Delta_{\epsilon}>0
such that if
\max_{0\leq i\leq K-1}(t_{i+1}-t_{i})\leq\Delta_{\epsilon}
,
then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{Z}}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{K-1}\frac{t_{i+1}-t_{i}}{t_{i}}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{y}_{t_{i}},\mathbf{x}}\|\widehat{\mathbf{b}}(1-t_{i},\mathbf{y}_{t_{i}},\mathbf{x})-\nabla\log p_{1-t_{i}}(\mathbf{y}_{t_{i}}|\mathbf{x})\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq(1-2T)\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{Z}}\left[\frac{1}{1-2T}\int_{T}^{1-T}\frac{1}{t}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{y}_{t},\mathbf{x}}\|\widehat{\mathbf{b}}(1-t,\mathbf{y}_{t},\mathbf{x})-\nabla\log p_{1-t}(\mathbf{y}_{t}|\mathbf{x})\|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\right]+\epsilon

\displaystyle\lesssim n^{-\frac{2\beta}{d_{\mathcal{X}}+d_{\mathcal{Y}}+2\beta}}\log^{19}n+\epsilon.

Specially, taking
\epsilon=n^{-\frac{2\beta}{d_{\mathcal{X}}+d_{\mathcal{Y}}+2\beta}}\log^{19}n
, there exists
\Delta_{\epsilon}=\Delta_{n}
such that if
\max_{0\leq i\leq K-1}(t_{i+1}-t_{i})\leq\Delta_{n}
, then we have

\displaystyle\penalty\penalty\penalty\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{Z}}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{K-1}\frac{t_{i+1}-t_{i}}{t_{i}}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{y}_{t_{i}},\mathbf{x}}\|\widehat{\mathbf{b}}(1-t_{i},\mathbf{y}_{t_{i}},\mathbf{x})-\nabla\log p_{1-t_{i}}(\mathbf{y}_{t_{i}}|\mathbf{x})\|^{2}\right)

(12)

\displaystyle\lesssim n^{-\frac{2\beta}{d_{\mathcal{X}}+d_{\mathcal{Y}}+2\beta}}\log^{19}n.

Using (
12
), we can bound
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{Z}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\mathcal{W}_{2}(\widetilde{p}_{T}^{B}(\cdot|\mathbf{x}),p_{T}^{B}(\cdot|\mathbf{x}))]
as the following theorem.

Theorem 3.4
.

Suppose that assumptions of Theorem
3.3
hold. By choosing
\max_{0\leq i\leq K-1}(t_{i+1}-t_{i})=\mathcal{O}\left(\min\{\Delta_{n},n^{-\frac{3\beta}{d_{\mathcal{X}}+d_{\mathcal{Y}}+2\beta}}\}\right)
, then we have

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{Z}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\mathcal{W}_{2}(\widetilde{p}_{T}^{B}(\cdot|\mathbf{x}),p_{T}^{B}(\cdot|\mathbf{x}))]\lesssim n^{-\frac{\beta}{d_{\mathcal{X}}+d_{\mathcal{Y}}+2\beta}}\log^{\frac{19}{2}}n.

3.1.2
Bound
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\mathcal{W}_{2}(p_{T}^{B}(\cdot|\mathbf{x}),p_{0}(\cdot|\mathbf{x}))]

In this subsection, we present the upper bound for
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\mathcal{W}_{2}(p_{T}^{B}(\cdot|\mathbf{x}),p_{0}(\cdot|\mathbf{x}))]
. This error originates from the process of early stopping. We have the following lemma.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\lfloor n^{\frac{1}{d_{\mathcal{X}}+d_{\mathcal{Y}}+2\beta}}\rfloor+1\lesssim n^{\frac{1}{d_{\mathcal{X}}+d_{\mathcal{Y}}+2\beta}}
,
T=\mathcal{M}^{-C_{T}}
,
and
C_{T}=2\beta
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\mathcal{W}_{2}(p_{T}^{B}(\cdot|\mathbf{x}),p_{0}(\cdot|\mathbf{x}))]\lesssim n^{-\frac{\beta}{d_{\mathcal{X}}+d_{\mathcal{Y}}+2\beta}}.

3.1.3
Bound
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{Z}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\mathcal{W}_{2}(\widetilde{p}_{T}^{B}(\cdot|\mathbf{x}),p_{0}(\cdot|\mathbf{x}))]

Theorem 3.6

(Convergence Rate of Conditional Diffusion Model)
.

Suppose that Assumptions
3.1
-
3.4
hold,
and the conditional score estimator
\mathbf{b}
is structured as introduced in Theorem

3.3
.
By choosing
\mathcal{M}
,
m
,
C_{T}
, introduced in Theorem
3.3
and
\max_{0\leq i\leq K-1}(t_{i+1}-t_{i})=\mathcal{O}\left(\min\{\Delta_{n},n^{-\frac{3\beta}{d_{\mathcal{X}}+d_{\mathcal{Y}}+2\beta}}\}\right)
,
we have

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{Z}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\mathcal{W}_{2}(\widetilde{p}_{T}^{B}(\cdot|\mathbf{x}),p_{0}(\cdot|\mathbf{x}))]\lesssim n^{-\frac{\beta}{d_{\mathcal{X}}+d_{\mathcal{Y}}+2\beta}}\log^{\frac{19}{2}}n.

Remark 3.3
.

Theorem
3.6
establishes a sharp
end-to-end convergence rate for conditional diffusion models. This advances the literature by improving upon earlier analyses that do not provide end-to-end guarantees, including
Chen et al. 2023a
;
Conforti et al. 2023
;
Lee et al. 2022
;
Lee et al. 2023
;
Benton et al. 2023
;
Li et al. 2023
;
Gao et al. 2023
. In comparison to existing end-to-end results, our convergence bound represents a significant refinement over works such as
Chen et al. 2023b
;
Jiao et al. 2025
, which relies on
Lipschitz-continuous score networks and consequently yield suboptimal rates. Our theoretical guarantees are comparable to those in
Oko et al. 2023
;
Jiao et al. 2024
;
Fu et al. 2024
; however, it is important to note that
Fu et al. 2024
does not incorporate the numerical discretization error of the reverse process, which is explicitly addressed in our analysis.

3.2
Convergence of Bootstrap

In this section, we present the main theoretical result on the convergence of our deep bootstrap method. First, we establish the convergence rate in Wasserstein distance between the centered estimator
\widehat{R}(\mathbf{x}):=\widehat{f}(\mathbf{x})-f_{0}(\mathbf{x})
and its bootstrap counterpart

\widehat{R}^{*}(\mathbf{x}):=\widehat{f}^{*}(\mathbf{x})-\widehat{f}(\mathbf{x})
, as shown in Theorem
3.7
. Building on this result, we further establish the coverage guarantee for the confidence interval constructed using the deep bootstrap procedure.

Theorem 3.7

(Convergence Rate of Bootstrap)
.

Assume that the conditions of
Theorem
3.6
hold.
We denote by

\widehat{R}(\mathbf{x}):=\widehat{f}(\mathbf{x})-f_{0}(\mathbf{x})
and

\widehat{R}^{*}(\mathbf{x}):=\widehat{f}^{*}(\mathbf{x})-\widehat{f}(\mathbf{x})
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{D}^{*},\mathcal{T}^{*},\mathcal{Z}^{*}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}\left[\mathcal{W}_{1}\left(\widehat{R}(\mathbf{x}),\widehat{R}^{*}(\mathbf{x})\right)\right]\lesssim n^{-\frac{\beta}{d_{\mathcal{X}}+d_{\mathcal{Y}}+2\beta}}\log^{\frac{19}{2}}n.

With Theorem
3.7
, we can conclude that
\widehat{R}^{*}(\mathbf{x})
is
\mathcal{W}_{1}
-consistency of
\widehat{R}(\mathbf{x})
; see Chapter 3 of
Shao and Tu 2012
.
Now, we can establish the coverage guarantee for the confidence interval defined in (
11
), as given in the following theorem.

Theorem 3.8

(Coverage Guarantee of Confidence Interval)
.

Assume that the conditions of
Theorem
3.6
hold. We have

\mathbb{P}\left(f_{0}(\mathbf{x})\in\left[\widehat{f}(\mathbf{x})-(\widehat{H}^{*})^{-1}(1-\alpha/2),\widehat{f}(\mathbf{x})-(\widehat{H}^{*})^{-1}(\alpha/2)\right]\right)\longrightarrow 1-\alpha.

4
Numerical Experiments

In this section, we validate the efficiency of the proposed deep bootstrap method through a series of numerical experiments.
Throughout the experiments, we consider the
nonparametric regression model with a standard normal noise:

\mathbf{Y}=f_{0}(\mathbf{X})+\epsilon,\quad\epsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1),

where the covariate vector is multi-dimensional and the response variable is of 1 dimension. To comprehensively assess the regression capability of our deep bootstrap method across different scenarios, we examine bounded and unbounded domains for the covariate vector respectively. Notably, unlike the assumptions made in the theoretical analysis, we do not impose a bounded domain on the response variable in our experimental setup. This choice enables us to evaluate the robustness and generalizability of the proposed method under more realistic and diverse conditions.

Several metrics are adopted to evaluate the performance of the experimental results. Firstly, we employ the coverage probability (CP). Note that the CP we use in the experiments are defined to be

\text{CP}:=\frac{\#\{\mathbf{x}\in\text{Test}:f_{0}(\mathbf{x})\in\left[\alpha_{1}(\mathbf{x}),\alpha_{2}(\mathbf{x})\right]\}}{N_{\text{Test}}},

where
N_{\text{Test}}
denotes the size of the test set, and
\alpha_{1}(\mathbf{x}),\alpha_{2}(\mathbf{x})
represent the endpoints of the confidence interval defined in (
11
). According to Theorem
3.8
, for a fixed confidence level
1-\alpha
, the CP converges to
1-\alpha
as the sample size grows. Secondly, two mean square errors (MSE) are introduced, respectively denoted by
\text{MSE}_{\text{org}}
(representing the original MSE of the training step) and
\text{MSE}_{\text{b}}
(representing MSE of the bootstrap step). The two measure the accuracy of the base-model training step and the bootstrap step. The third one is the average interval length, which quantifies the length of the confidence interval averaged over the test set. All the aforementioned metrics are specified and summarized in Table
1
.

Table 1:
Several metrics

Metrics

Mathematical formulation

CP

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N_{\text{Test}}}\sum\limits_{\mathbf{x}\in\text{Test}}\mathbf{I}\Big(f_{0}(\mathbf{x})\in[\alpha_{1}(\mathbf{x}),\alpha_{2}(\mathbf{x})]\Big)

\text{MSE}_{\text{org}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N_{\text{Test}}}\sum\limits_{\mathbf{x}\in\text{Test}}\left|\widehat{f}(\mathbf{x})-f_{0}(\mathbf{x})\right|^{2}

\text{MSE}_{\text{b}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N_{\text{Test}}}\sum\limits_{\mathbf{x}\in\text{Test}}\frac{1}{\mathcal{B}}\sum\limits_{b=1}^{\mathcal{B}}\left|\widehat{f}^{*}_{b}(\mathbf{x})-\widehat{f}(\mathbf{x})\right|^{2}

Interval Length

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N_{\text{Test}}}\sum\limits_{\mathbf{x}\in\text{Test}}\left(\alpha_{2}(\mathbf{x})-\alpha_{1}(\mathbf{x})\right)

During the implementation, we fix the confidence level
1-\alpha
to be 0.95 (i.e.
\alpha=0.05
). The test sample size is held constant as 2500, with the total number of train and test samples varied only through the proportion of the test sample size. The number of epochs for training the base model is determined adaptively based on the observed decay of training error. This epoch count is then maintained throughout the bootstrap model-training procedure. For the bootstrap ensemble size
\mathcal{B}
, which serves as a hyperparameter, we would normally adjust it flexibly according to model performance. However, to facilitate comparative analysis across experiments, we fix
\mathcal{B}
at an empirically determined value of 200 throughout all the experiments. To see all the details of experiment settings, please refer to Table
4
.

Experiments are carried out with dimension of the covariate vector chosen to be 5 and 10. In the 5-dimensional scenario, the following two targets of bounded and unbounded domains are considered. Both targets are designed to be maximally complex and representative.

(I)

\mathbf{X}\sim U([0,1]^{d_{\mathcal{X}}}),\ \epsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
with
f_{0}
defined by

f_{0}(\mathbf{x})=(2x_{1}-x_{2}+1)^{2}+|x_{3}-5|+\exp(x_{4}+\frac{x_{5}}{2}).

(II)

\mathbf{X}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\textbf{I}_{d_{\mathcal{X}}}),\ \epsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
with
f_{0}
defined by

f_{0}(\mathbf{x})=(2x_{1}-1)^{2}-x_{2}^{3}+\exp(\frac{1}{10}(x_{3}+x_{4}+x_{5})).

The latter two functions serve to demonstrate the applicability of our method to generic complex functions as well as functions exhibiting strong oscillatory behavior.

(I)

with linear target
f_{0}(\mathbf{x})=W\mathbf{x}+b
, whose weight
W
and bias
b
are randomly drawn from the uniform distribution over [-1,1].

with
f_{0}
defined by

f_{0}(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{i=0}^{1}\left[\sin(2x_{5i+1}+x_{5i+2})+\frac{1}{2}\left(\cos(x_{5i+3})+x_{5i+4}^{2}\right)x_{5i+5}\right].

Based on the experimental results presented in Table
2
and
3
, we observe that the key index CP consistently approaches the ideal value of 0.95 across various target function configurations, achieving low MSEs simultaneously. This empirically validates both the theoretical results of Theorem
3.7
and the feasibility of the deep bootstrap method. While our training procedure and hyperparameter settings may not achieve the global optimality, we empirically claim that the learning difficulty increases with higher dimensionality of the target covariate vector as well as greater functional complexity. These factors correlate with reduced precision in the CP. Furthermore, our results indicate that increasing the training sample size
n
and expanding the bootstrap ensemble size
\mathcal{B}

both contribute to enhanced accuracy of the CP. We therefore recommend practitioners to conduct adaptive hyperparameter exploration based on the dimension, specific functional forms and computational cost to achieve an optimal calculation.

5
Conclusion

This work presents a deep bootstrap method for statistical inference in nonparametric regression, built upon conditional diffusion models. By harnessing the expressive power of diffusion models, our approach goes beyond traditional bootstrap methods by unifying nonparametric estimation and resampling, achieving efficiency in
high-dimensional and potentially multimodal settings.
We provide rigorous theoretical guarantees, including sharp convergence rates for both the conditional diffusion model and the deep bootstrap procedure, as well as consistency of the resulting confidence intervals. Together, these results offer a strong theoretical basis for integrating generative modeling into bootstrap-based inference frameworks.
Future research may explore extending the deep bootstrap framework to more general settings, such as time series analysis and broader classes of nonparametric models. Additional directions include applying the method to diverse domains within statistics and machine learning, and incorporating recent advances in diffusion models to further improve empirical performance and robustness.

In this appendix, we provide detailed proofs for the theoretical results presented in the paper. Section
A
establishes an approximation error bound for the score network, while Section
B
derives the corresponding statistical error bound. In Section
C
, we bound
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{Z}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}\left[\mathcal{W}_{2}(\widetilde{p}_{T}^{B}(\cdot|\mathbf{x}),p_{0}(\cdot|\mathbf{x}))\right]
. Section
D
presents the convergence analysis of the proposed deep bootstrap procedure. Finally, auxiliary lemmas used throughout the proofs are deferred to Section
E
.

Appendix A
Approximation Error

Building on the preliminary lemmas in Sections
E.1
-
E.2
, we now proceed to the proof of Lemma
3.1
by following
Oko et al. 2023
;
Fu et al. 2024
.
Recall that the conditional score function
\nabla\log p_{t}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})
can be rewritten as

\nabla\log p_{t}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})=\frac{\nabla p_{t}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})}{p_{t}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})}.

We approximate the numerator and denominator separately. The construction of the approximations to the numerator and denominator is similar. In the following, we focus on the approximation of
p_{t}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})
. The procedure for approximating
p_{t}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})
is outlined as follows:

Clipping the integral interval.

Recall that

p_{t}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}}}p_{0}(\mathbf{y}_{1}|\mathbf{x})\mathbf{I}_{\{\|\mathbf{y}_{1}\|_{\infty}\leq 1\}}\cdot\underbrace{\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}}(2\pi)^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|\mathbf{y}-m_{t}\mathbf{y}_{1}\|^{2}}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}\right)}_{\text{Transition Kernel}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}_{1}.

According to Lemma
E.2
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that for any
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}}
,
\mathbf{x}\in[-1,1]^{d_{\mathcal{X}}}
,

\left|p_{t}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})-\int_{A_{\mathbf{y}}}p_{0}(\mathbf{y}_{1}|\mathbf{x})\mathbf{I}_{\{\|\mathbf{y}_{1}\|_{\infty}\leq 1\}}\cdot\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}}(2\pi)^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|\mathbf{y}-m_{t}\mathbf{y}_{1}\|^{2}}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}\right)\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}_{1}\right|\lesssim\frac{\epsilon}{t^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}}},

where
A_{\mathbf{y}}=\prod_{i=1}^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}}a_{i,\mathbf{y}}
with
a_{i,\mathbf{y}}=\left[\frac{y_{i}-C\sigma_{t}\sqrt{\log\epsilon^{-1}}}{m_{t}},\frac{y_{i}+C\sigma_{t}\sqrt{\log\epsilon^{-1}}}{m_{t}}\right]
.
This implies that we only need to approximate the integral over
A_{\mathbf{y}}
sufficiently.

Approximating
p_{0}(\mathbf{y}_{1}|\mathbf{x})
.

Recall that the target density function
p_{0}(\mathbf{y}_{1}|\mathbf{x})
is assumed to be Hölder continuous, as stated in Assumption
3.2
.
Therefore, we can utilize the Taylor polynomial
f_{\rm{Taylor}}(\mathbf{y}_{1},\mathbf{x})
to approximate
p_{0}(\mathbf{y}_{1}|\mathbf{x})
.
Consequently, to approximate
p_{t}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})
,
we derive an approximation in the form of

\int_{A_{\mathbf{y}}}f_{\rm{Taylor}}(\mathbf{y}_{1},\mathbf{x})\mathbf{I}_{\{\|\mathbf{y}_{1}\|_{\infty}\leq 1\}}\cdot\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}}(2\pi)^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|\mathbf{y}-m_{t}\mathbf{y}_{1}\|^{2}}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}\right)\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}_{1}.

This analysis is detailed in Section
A.1
.

Approximating the transition kernel.

Although the Taylor polynomial
f_{\rm{Taylor}}(\mathbf{y}_{1},\mathbf{x})
can be implemented using a neural network, integrating over
\mathbf{y}_{1}
remains challenging. To address this, we introduce the Taylor polynomial
f_{\rm{Taylor}}^{\rm{kernel}}(t,\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}_{1})
to approximate the exponential transition kernel, resulting in the following approximation:

\int_{A_{\mathbf{y}}}f_{\rm{Taylor}}(\mathbf{y}_{1},\mathbf{x})f_{\rm{Taylor}}^{\rm{kernel}}(t,\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}_{1})\mathbf{I}_{\{\|\mathbf{y}_{1}\|_{\infty}\leq 1\}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}_{1}.

(13)

See Section
A.2
for more details.
A similar approximation scheme using local polynomials can also be applied to
\nabla p_{t}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})
.

Approximating the integral via ReLU neural networks.

In (
13
), the product
f_{\rm{Taylor}}(\mathbf{y}_{1},\mathbf{x})

\cdot f_{\rm{Taylor}}^{\rm{kernel}}(t,\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}_{1})
is a polynomial, thus its integration can be computed explicitly. Consequently, we use ReLU neural networks to approximate the local polynomials, as outlined in Section
A.3
.

Finally, combining the above discussions, we derive the error bound for approximating
\nabla\log p_{t}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})
using a ReLU neural network, as detailed in Section
A.4
.

A.1
Approximating
p_{0}
via local polynomials

In this section, we approximate
p_{0}
via local polynomials, which is a key step for approximating the true conditional score function
\nabla\log p_{t}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})
. We give the following lemma.

Lemma A.1

(Approximating
p_{0}
via local polynomials)
.

Suppose Assumptions
3.1
-
3.2
hold. Let
\mathcal{M}\gg 1
, there exists a function
p_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x})
such that

\left|p_{0}(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{x})-p_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x})\right|\lesssim\mathcal{M}^{-\beta},\penalty\ \mathbf{y}\in[-1,1]^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}},\mathbf{x}\in[-1,1]^{d_{\mathcal{X}}}.

Proof.

We denote

f(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}):=p_{0}(2\mathbf{y}-1|2\mathbf{x}-1),\penalty\ \mathbf{y}\in[0,1]^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}},\mathbf{x}\in[0,1]^{d_{\mathcal{X}}}.

By Assumption
3.2
, we know that
\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\beta}([0,1]^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}}\times[0,1]^{d_{\mathcal{X}}})}\leq 2^{r}R
.
For any
\mathbf{m}=(m_{1},m_{2},\cdots,m_{d_{\mathcal{Y}}})^{\top}\in[\mathcal{M}]^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}}:=\{0,1,\cdots,\mathcal{M}\}^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}}
and
\mathbf{n}=(n_{1},n_{2},\cdots,n_{d_{\mathcal{X}}})^{\top}\in[\mathcal{M}]^{d_{\mathcal{X}}}:=\{0,1,\cdots,\mathcal{M}\}^{d_{\mathcal{X}}}
, we define

\displaystyle\psi_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}):

\displaystyle=\mathbf{I}_{\left\{\mathbf{y}\in\left(\frac{\mathbf{m}-1}{\mathcal{M}},\frac{\mathbf{m}}{\mathcal{M}}\right],\penalty\ \mathbf{x}\in\left(\frac{\mathbf{n}-1}{\mathcal{M}},\frac{\mathbf{n}}{\mathcal{M}}\right]\right\}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}}\mathbf{I}_{\left\{y_{i}\in\left(\frac{m_{i}-1}{\mathcal{M}},\frac{m_{i}}{\mathcal{M}}\right]\right\}}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{d_{\mathcal{X}}}\mathbf{I}_{\left\{x_{i}\in\left(\frac{n_{i}-1}{\mathcal{M}},\frac{n_{i}}{\mathcal{M}}\right]\right\}}.

Note that the functions
\{\psi_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\}_{\mathbf{m}\in[\mathcal{M}]^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}},\mathbf{n}\in[\mathcal{M}]^{d_{\mathcal{M}}}}
form a partition of unity of the domain
[0,1]^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}}\times[0,1]^{d_{\mathcal{X}}}
, i.e.,

\sum_{\mathbf{m}\in[\mathcal{M}]^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}},\mathbf{n}\in[\mathcal{M}]^{d_{\mathcal{X}}}}\psi_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x})\equiv 1,\penalty\ \mathbf{y}\in[0,1]^{d_{\mathcal{Y}}},\mathbf{x}\in[0,1]^{d_{\mathcal{X}}}.

Denote

p_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{\gamma}}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}):=\psi_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x})\left(\mathbf{y}-\frac{\mathbf{m}}{\mathcal{M}}\right)^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\left(\mathbf{x}-\frac{\mathbf{n}}{\mathcal{M}}\right)^{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}

and
